A printer is really a fact about the workshop,\n  not the project, so `~/.config/nurb/config.toml` takes the same schema and covers every\n  project on the machine; `printer.toml` overrides it where they disagree.\n\n```toml\n[part]\nmin_wall = 1.0\nforward = [-1, 0, 0]\n\n[accepted]\nsliver = 6\n```\n\n### Variants\n\nA catalog entry that is the same function at different numbers is a variant, not a new\nfile. It goes in the same fence, and `build`, `check`, `card` and `export` all walk it\nexactly as they walk the part's own defaults, so it gets its own STL, its own baselines\nand its own line in the AUTO block.\n\n```toml\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2]\nnote = \"The same shelf sized for the wide bin: three columns instead of two.\"\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.params]\ngrid_x = 3\nbracket_count = 6\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\nsliver = 26\n```\n\nThe test is whether it is the same geometry. A wider cradle on the same J is a variant.\nA different mechanism is a part, however similar the slab looks.\n\n`note` is one sentence saying why the variant exists, written in the user's words rather than the doctrine's, because the viewer shows it next to the variant and its reader is whoever is about to print the thing. The params are the how and never the why: `diagonal = true` explains nothing to someone who has not read the doctrine, and \"prints tilted so it needs no supports\" does.\n\n## Measurements\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off is not geometry, it is dimensions. A wrong number produces a\nperfect model of the wrong object, and nothing downstream can catch it.\n\n**Ask for a measurement. Never improvise one.** A guessed clearance that happens to\nbuild is worse than a failure, because it prints. Record what you are told in\n`measurements.toml` at the project root, with how it was obtained, and read it with\n`measured()`:\n\n```toml\n[shelf_depth]\nvalue = 340\nunit = \"mm\"\nhow = \"tape measure, freezer middle shelf, 2026-07-20\"\n```\n\n```python\nfrom nurb import measured\n\ndepth = measured(\"shelf_depth\")\n```\n\nAn unknown name raises, naming what is on file. That failure is the point: it is the\nmoment to ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n\n**A published number is a measurement, not a guess.** When the part mates with a manufactured product or a published standard, a VESA mount, a gridfinity bin, a camera thread, the number already exists in a datasheet, and making somebody caliper a hole pattern a standards body fixed is friction wearing rigor's clothes. Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. Pixels carry no millimetres, so a dimension read off a photo is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n\n**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n\n**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\nThe danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\nmonths later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\nnext person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\nsomebody picks up a caliper.\n\nA measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\namortized it over sixteen parts.\n\n## Assemblies\n\nEvery rule above judges one solid being manufactured. An assembly judges solids being\nused together: a door on its mount, a lid over the machine it covers. The failure it\nexists for built clean, checked clean, exported watertight and printed beautifully,\nthen jammed at 45 degrees of its swing, because the collision between two correct\nparts existed only in the physical world. No per-solid rule can have an opinion about\nmotion.\n\nThe contract mirrors a part's. An assembly is a function in `parts/` whose keyword\ndefaults are its parameters; what it returns is placed solids rather than one solid.\n`use(name)` builds a sibling part where it was modelled, `hinge(solid, axis, through,\nat)` declares a revolute joint and poses it, and `obstacle(solid, name)` adds what the\nparts mount into: the machine, the wall, the shelf above. The joint angle is an\nordinary keyword default, so the viewer's slider swings it live, and the slider ends\nexactly where the declared range does.\n\n**The declared range is a claim, and `nurb check` audits it** the way min_wall audits\na wall: each hinge sweeps through it against everything else in the scene, and a\nfinding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\nthe design needs, never what happens to pass. A door that must stay open by gravity\nneeds past 90 degrees; declaring (0, 90) because that sweeps clean is writing the test\nto match the bug.\n\n**Nothing full width may cross the pivot line.** The mount that carries a hinge has to\nreach the pin from somewhere, so some of it always lives above or behind the pin, and\nany full-width material on the moving part that reaches past the pivot sweeps straight\ninto it. The door that jammed at 45 did so against its own top edge, carried past the\npin to support the knuckles; the knuckles got narrow tabs instead, sized to pass\nbeside the mount, and the panel now starts at the pivot line.\n\n**Obstacles are boxes and prisms, never thin hand-drawn profiles.** Three nearly\ncollinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\nsolid with chunks of the other operand: measured once as a 152,000mm3 phantom\ncollision at a pose that was actually clear. The sweep refuses any intersection that\nis not a subset of its inputs and names the solid to fix, but the fix is to model the\nobstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n\n**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\ntwo faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\nIn plastic a zero-clearance pass is already a bind. The declared range should carry\nthe same honesty about clearance that a tongue's `fit` carries about width.\n\n**The stop the sweep finds can be the detent the design wants.** The door that\nmotivated all of this rests open at 240 degrees against the back of its own mount,\nheld there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\nto engineer away. Read a jam before redesigning around it.\n\n**Never trust a swing worked out by hand.** The same hinge was hand-modelled three\ntimes and gave three answers, one of them \"impossible\"; the wrong one ran a convex\ntest against a non-convex profile, which is exactly the kind of quiet modelling error\na kernel boolean cannot make. The sweep is the authority, and it is cheap.\n\nOne hinge sweeps at a time; the others hold their pose, which is the conservative\nreading of a mechanism you can only move one hand at a time. Printability rules stay\noff assemblies entirely: each part already answered them alone, and overhang measured\non an assembled scene reports confident nonsense.\n\n## Verification\n\n\"It built\" is not verification. `nurb verify` runs the machine-checkable part of\nthis list: one solid per configuration, every count flexed upward, the rules clean,\nand the card agreeing with the geometry. `--report` writes the verdict into\n`build/renders/<part>.verify.md` with the renders that back it beside it, one still\nper finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n\nBefore presenting a part:\n\n1. **Flex the driving parameters up as well as down**, for example 4 to 6 to 2 to 4.\n   Growth is what catches a frozen selector; shrinking alone passes a broken part.\n2. **Check fit-critical faces by coordinate** after any polish or resize. `nurb inspect`\n   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. Which faces are\n   fit-critical is still yours to know, which is why this is not something `nurb verify`\n   can run for you.\n3. **Confirm the solid count**, which is almost always one.\n4. **Run `nurb check`.** Zero findings is the bar, and a finding fired at a part that\n   prints fine is a bug in the rule, not something to accept quietly.\n5. **Predict a baseline before you look at it.** Working out what the sliver count\n   should be from the polish exclusions turns the number into a test of the rule instead\n   of something to write down.\n6. **Render it and actually look.** `nurb render <part>` writes a PNG, `--section\n   z:4mm` cuts it open where outside views cannot reach, and `nurb inspect --render`\n   stands a camera at every finding with the guilty face painted. A part can pass\n   every numeric check and still be visibly wrong.\n\n===== API =====\n  from nurb import *  gives a part file all of build123d, plus:\n\n  part(fn)\n      Mark a function as a part.\n  reject(message, param=None)\n      Refuse to build a configuration the part knows cannot work.\n  is_convex(edge, f1, f2)\n      True if the material folds away from itself across this edge.\n  concave_edges(shape)\n      Every concave edge, which is where polish is forbidden and stress collects.\n  measured(name, start=None)\n      A measured value, in mm.\n  polish(shape, edges, size)\n      Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.\n  crown(wall, radius=None)\n      Round the top of a closed perimeter wall with one smooth bead, on request.\n  stand(shape, tilt=45.0, axis=Axis((0, 0, 0), (0, 1, 0)), facet=2.0, fins=True)\n      Tilt a part for diagonal printing and shave a flat for it to stand on.\n  counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0)\n      The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n  assembly(fn)\n      Mark a function as an assembly. Its keyword defaults are parameters, exactly\n  use(name, **overrides)\n      Build a sibling part by name and return its solid, placed where it was modelled.\n  hinge(solid, axis, through, at=0.0, name=None, step=3.0)\n      Declare that this solid rotates about `axis`, and pose it at `at` degrees.\n  obstacle(solid, name='an obstacle')\n      Context geometry: the wall, the machine, the shelf above.\n\n  And it replaces these build123d names with its own. Read the summary:\n\n  chamfer(objects: build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.zero_d.Vertex | collections.abc.Iterable[build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.zero_d.Vertex], length: float, length2: float | None = None, angle: float | None = None, reference: build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.two_d.Face | None = None) -> build123d.topology.composite.Sketch | build123d.topology.composite.Part\n      Generic Operation: chamfer\n  import_stl(file_name, units=None)\n      A closed, flat-faced STL as a real solid, or a refusal that names `nurb scan`.\n\n  From build123d, because the doctrine mandates it:\n\n  new_edges(*objects: 'Shape', combined: 'Shape') -> 'ShapeList[Edge]'\n      Given a sequence of shapes and the combination of those shapes, find the newly added edges\n\n  `nurb rules` is when to reach for these. `nurb inspect <part>` measures a\n  built one in the same units the rules report.\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"Bash","output":[],"output_for_prompt":"a shipped profile (`profile = \"bambu_a1_mini\"`) and can\n  override any check setting machine-wide. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-7333225b-fe9d-4058-bc66-c1eaaf87a222-3","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"# nurb design doctrine\n\nPrinted via `nurb rules`. This file is the single source; harness files point at it\nrather than repeating it.\n\nThese are standing rules for FDM-printed parts, and several are vetoes rather than\npreferences. Most of them are physics, so they outlive any particular CAD kernel. The\nones that are specific to OCCT are marked as such and were measured, not reasoned.\n\n## The part contract\n\n```python\nfrom nurb import *\n\n@part\ndef dispenser(width=80.0, height=120.0, wall=2.0, draft=False):\n    body = Box(width, height, wall)\n    if draft:\n        return body\n    bed = body.bounding_box().min.Z\n    keep = body.edges().filter_by(lambda e: e.bounding_box().min.Z > bed)\n    return polish(body, keep, 1.0)\n```\n\nKeyword defaults are the parameters. That one declaration feeds the agent, the CLI, the\nviewer, the tests, and any future configurator. Never add a parallel `PARAMS` dict; the\ntwo would drift.\n\n**Write a continuous dimension as a float.** The type of a default is the only thing\nthat says whether a value is a count or a measurement, and the viewer reads it: an `int`\ndefault gets a slider that steps by one, a `float` default one that moves continuously.\n`bracket_count=4` is a count and `chamfer_size=1.0` is a millimetre, so the trailing\n`.0` is doing work. Nothing else has to be declared, and there is no annotation to keep\nin sync, because the type is already there in the signature.\n\n`draft` is optional and injected by the runtime, never passed by a caller. When it is\ntrue, skip the polish pass. It is worth about 20% on a real part, not the 18x a cube\nsuggests.\n\nReturn one solid. A function that returns two loose bodies still reports a plausible\nbounding box and still exports, so nothing downstream catches it.\n\n**When a configuration cannot work, refuse it with `reject`, never a bare raise.** A holder whose hole is narrower than the tool it holds should not build, and `reject(\"drill_hole_width 14 is under the 14.27mm pin vise plus print shrink: raise it above 14.77\", param=\"drill_hole_width\")` is how a part says so. The message states what is wrong and what value fixes it; `param` names the offending parameter so the viewer marks its slider. The viewer presents a refusal as a limit of the design, in amber with the last good geometry still on screen, where a bare `ValueError` arrives as a red traceback that reads as the part being broken. Guard only what would print wrong or not work at all; taste stays a slider.\n\n## Printability\n\n- **Minimum wall 2 to 3mm**, except where fit geometry says otherwise. Fit-critical\n  dimensions are already tuned; do not round them for tidiness.\n- **Print support-free. No floating features.** Parts print bottom down, building up in\n  +z, so every functional feature is grounded. A vertical extrusion or a vertical\n  through hole is self-supporting. A shelf, loop, or band floated partway up with a gap\n  underneath is not.\n- **A counterbore prints mouth toward the bed, and that floats its ceiling.** The shelf the screw head bears on is laid flat over the open pocket, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air; the `hole_ceiling` finding is this exact case, and it appears on any hole rising out of a bridged roof, not just screw pockets. Never answer it with supports inside a pocket nobody can clean: cut the hole with `counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth)`, which steps the transition through two sacrificial bridge layers, a slot bridged chord-to-chord across the pocket and the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first, so each layer spans only what the one before it laid and the whole stack prints support-free. Reach for it whenever a design wants a screw head, a nut, or any wider recess on the bed side of a hole.\n- **Corbel rule.** Grounded does not mean a column to the bed. Support-free means no\n  layer overhanging past 45 degrees. Where material would run to the plate only to\n  satisfy printing, carry the feature on a 45 degree underside instead: a short vertical\n  tip of 4 to 6mm below the floor at the front face, then a 45 degree plane falling back\n  into the body. Every layer then overhangs by at most half a bead and bonds to the wall\n  behind it.\n- **Corbels are always exactly 45 degrees**, the same as every other facet. Never\n  steepen one or match it to a ratio. Let the landing point fall where 45 degrees puts\n  it. Mismatched facet angles are a veto: every facet on the part, cosmetic or\n  structural or corbel, has to read as one system.\n- **Elephant's foot is a slicer setting**, not a CAD problem. Do not model around it.\n\n### Fasteners\n\n**A published standard is not a guess.** Measurements says never improvise a dimension, and a screw is the one place that rule gets misread: nobody has to measure an M3 cap screw, because ISO fixed its head at 5.5mm across in every box you will ever open. Take these from the table and spend the question you saved on something the table cannot answer.\n\n| Thread | Clearance hole, close | medium | Cap head dia | Cap head height | Nut across flats | across corners | Nut thickness |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| M2 | 2.2 | 2.4 | 3.8 | 2.0 | 4.0 | 4.4 | 1.6 |\n| M2.5 | 2.7 | 2.9 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 5.0 | 5.5 | 2.0 |\n| M3 | 3.2 | 3.4 | 5.5 | 3.0 | 5.5 | 6.1 | 2.4 |\n| M4 | 4.3 | 4.5 | 7.0 | 4.0 | 7.0 | 7.7 | 3.2 |\n| M5 | 5.3 | 5.5 | 8.5 | 5.0 | 8.0 | 8.8 | 4.7 |\n| M6 | 6.4 | 6.6 | 10.0 | 6.0 | 10.0 | 11.1 | 5.2 |\n| M8 | 8.4 | 9.0 | 13.0 | 8.0 | 13.0 | 14.4 | 6.8 |\n\nClearance is ISO 273, cap head is ISO 4762 socket head, nut is ISO 4032 hex. In mm. Across corners is the one a round pocket has to clear, and it is the number people reach for the wrong column of: an M3 nut is 5.5 across the flats and will not go into a 5.5 hole.\n\n**Reach for the medium column.** A printed hole comes out under its modelled size, by a few tenths that vary with the machine, the material and how fast the perimeter was laid, so the close column is a fit you have to earn with a coupon rather than one you can assume. Medium already carries that much slack, which is why it is the default here and close is the exception a fit coupon has justified.\n\n**Give a screw head its pocket through `counterbore`, never a plain wider hole.** Head diameter plus about 0.4, head height plus a layer, and the pocket faces the bed with the shaft rising out of it: that is the shape the table above is sized for, and it is the shape `counterbore` cuts.\n\n**A nut pocket is a hex prism, and `counterbore` cannot cut one.** It cuts a cylinder, so the pocket is modelled by hand: `extrude(RegularPolygon(across_corners / 2, 6), thickness + 0.2)` sized on the across-corners column, or a round pocket at across-corners plus 0.2 if the nut is free to spin. A hex takes about 0.2 of slack across the flats, and rotating the prism so a flat rather than a point faces up prints a cleaner socket. It floats its own ceiling exactly the way a screw head does and earns the same `hole_ceiling` finding, so the two sacrificial bridge layers still apply; that trick is the part of `counterbore` worth copying, and the cylinder is not.\n\n**Heat-set inserts are the exception that proves the rule.** Their boss diameter is set by the insert a particular vendor ships and not by any standard, the same nominal thread differs by half a millimetre between brands, and the number that matters is the one on the bag in the user's drawer. Ask, or measure one, and record it with `measured()`.\n\n## Print orientation\n\nParts print the way they are modelled, +z up, and for most parts that is the end of it. Orientation becomes a decision in exactly two situations: an overhang a corbel cannot carry without disfiguring the part, and a load that wraps a corner, where no flat orientation keeps every member strong.\n\n- **The two remedies for an overhang finding are a corbel and a tilt.** Reach for the corbel first: it is local, it keeps the part on its natural face, and it disappears into the design. Reach for the tilt when the whole part fights the bed. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n- **Layer bonds are about half strength**, measured near 50% of in-plane tensile for PLA and worse for ASA. A part loaded around a corner, which is every bracket and every L, has no flat orientation that keeps both legs loading in-plane: one of them always pulls its layers apart. Stood at 45 degrees neither does, and the load path never lies in a single weld plane. A tilt moves the weak plane rather than deleting it, so it is not automatic: a hook printed at an angle fails at the same load with the break somewhere else. It earns its keep on corners, not on straight pulls.\n- **`stand(part, tilt, axis, facet)` is the verb.** It rotates the part about a horizontal axis, seats it, and shaves the down corner flat, because a part standing on an unshaved corner meets the bed along a single extrusion line, and that line peels. The facet is 2mm minimum, measured across the flat. 45 degrees is the usual tilt and is not sacred: a shallow part stands at whatever angle brings everything inside the limit. Run the polish pass before standing, in the functional orientation.\n- **Stand a part on the corner that grounds every region, and prefer the lowest stance that does.** For an L that is the outside of its elbow, legs up in a V. The sign of the roll depends on which way the model faces, so check rather than reason: the same L rolled the other way stands on the end of one leg as a chevron, still grounded, still legal, but 60% taller on the same facet, and height on a facet is what spends the adhesion margin. The stance to refuse is the tent, elbow up, one leg's tip hanging in air, and it is easy to miss by eye because nothing about it looks steep: every face of the floating tip can sit at exactly 45 degrees, silent t\n\n... (output truncated) ...\n\na shipped profile (`profile = \"bambu_a1_mini\"`) and can\n  override any check setting machine-wide. A printer is really a fact about the workshop,\n  not the project, so `~/.config/nurb/config.toml` takes the same schema and covers every\n  project on the machine; `printer.toml` overrides it where they disagree.\n\n```toml\n[part]\nmin_wall = 1.0\nforward = [-1, 0, 0]\n\n[accepted]\nsliver = 6\n```\n\n### Variants\n\nA catalog entry that is the same function at different numbers is a variant, not a new\nfile. It goes in the same fence, and `build`, `check`, `card` and `export` all walk it\nexactly as they walk the part's own defaults, so it gets its own STL, its own baselines\nand its own line in the AUTO block.\n\n```toml\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2]\nnote = \"The same shelf sized for the wide bin: three columns instead of two.\"\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.params]\ngrid_x = 3\nbracket_count = 6\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\nsliver = 26\n```\n\nThe test is whether it is the same geometry. A wider cradle on the same J is a variant.\nA different mechanism is a part, however similar the slab looks.\n\n`note` is one sentence saying why the variant exists, written in the user's words rather than the doctrine's, because the viewer shows it next to the variant and its reader is whoever is about to print the thing. The params are the how and never the why: `diagonal = true` explains nothing to someone who has not read the doctrine, and \"prints tilted so it needs no supports\" does.\n\n## Measurements\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off is not geometry, it is dimensions. A wrong number produces a\nperfect model of the wrong object, and nothing downstream can catch it.\n\n**Ask for a measurement. Never improvise one.** A guessed clearance that happens to\nbuild is worse than a failure, because it prints. Record what you are told in\n`measurements.toml` at the project root, with how it was obtained, and read it with\n`measured()`:\n\n```toml\n[shelf_depth]\nvalue = 340\nunit = \"mm\"\nhow = \"tape measure, freezer middle shelf, 2026-07-20\"\n```\n\n```python\nfrom nurb import measured\n\ndepth = measured(\"shelf_depth\")\n```\n\nAn unknown name raises, naming what is on file. That failure is the point: it is the\nmoment to ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n\n**A published number is a measurement, not a guess.** When the part mates with a manufactured product or a published standard, a VESA mount, a gridfinity bin, a camera thread, the number already exists in a datasheet, and making somebody caliper a hole pattern a standards body fixed is friction wearing rigor's clothes. Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. Pixels carry no millimetres, so a dimension read off a photo is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n\n**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n\n**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\nThe danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\nmonths later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\nnext person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\nsomebody picks up a caliper.\n\nA measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\namortized it over sixteen parts.\n\n## Assemblies\n\nEvery rule above judges one solid being manufactured. An assembly judges solids being\nused together: a door on its mount, a lid over the machine it covers. The failure it\nexists for built clean, checked clean, exported watertight and printed beautifully,\nthen jammed at 45 degrees of its swing, because the collision between two correct\nparts existed only in the physical world. No per-solid rule can have an opinion about\nmotion.\n\nThe contract mirrors a part's. An assembly is a function in `parts/` whose keyword\ndefaults are its parameters; what it returns is placed solids rather than one solid.\n`use(name)` builds a sibling part where it was modelled, `hinge(solid, axis, through,\nat)` declares a revolute joint and poses it, and `obstacle(solid, name)` adds what the\nparts mount into: the machine, the wall, the shelf above. The joint angle is an\nordinary keyword default, so the viewer's slider swings it live, and the slider ends\nexactly where the declared range does.\n\n**The declared range is a claim, and `nurb check` audits it** the way min_wall audits\na wall: each hinge sweeps through it against everything else in the scene, and a\nfinding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\nthe design needs, never what happens to pass. A door that must stay open by gravity\nneeds past 90 degrees; declaring (0, 90) because that sweeps clean is writing the test\nto match the bug.\n\n**Nothing full width may cross the pivot line.** The mount that carries a hinge has to\nreach the pin from somewhere, so some of it always lives above or behind the pin, and\nany full-width material on the moving part that reaches past the pivot sweeps straight\ninto it. The door that jammed at 45 did so against its own top edge, carried past the\npin to support the knuckles; the knuckles got narrow tabs instead, sized to pass\nbeside the mount, and the panel now starts at the pivot line.\n\n**Obstacles are boxes and prisms, never thin hand-drawn profiles.** Three nearly\ncollinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\nsolid with chunks of the other operand: measured once as a 152,000mm3 phantom\ncollision at a pose that was actually clear. The sweep refuses any intersection that\nis not a subset of its inputs and names the solid to fix, but the fix is to model the\nobstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n\n**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\ntwo faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\nIn plastic a zero-clearance pass is already a bind. The declared range should carry\nthe same honesty about clearance that a tongue's `fit` carries about width.\n\n**The stop the sweep finds can be the detent the design wants.** The door that\nmotivated all of this rests open at 240 degrees against the back of its own mount,\nheld there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\nto engineer away. Read a jam before redesigning around it.\n\n**Never trust a swing worked out by hand.** The same hinge was hand-modelled three\ntimes and gave three answers, one of them \"impossible\"; the wrong one ran a convex\ntest against a non-convex profile, which is exactly the kind of quiet modelling error\na kernel boolean cannot make. The sweep is the authority, and it is cheap.\n\nOne hinge sweeps at a time; the others hold their pose, which is the conservative\nreading of a mechanism you can only move one hand at a time. Printability rules stay\noff assemblies entirely: each part already answered them alone, and overhang measured\non an assembled scene reports confident nonsense.\n\n## Verification\n\n\"It built\" is not verification. `nurb verify` runs the machine-checkable part of\nthis list: one solid per configuration, every count flexed upward, the rules clean,\nand the card agreeing with the geometry. `--report` writes the verdict into\n`build/renders/<part>.verify.md` with the renders that back it beside it, one still\nper finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n\nBefore presenting a part:\n\n1. **Flex the driving parameters up as well as down**, for example 4 to 6 to 2 to 4.\n   Growth is what catches a frozen selector; shrinking alone passes a broken part.\n2. **Check fit-critical faces by coordinate** after any polish or resize. `nurb inspect`\n   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. Which faces are\n   fit-critical is still yours to know, which is why this is not something `nurb verify`\n   can run for you.\n3. **Confirm the solid count**, which is almost always one.\n4. **Run `nurb check`.** Zero findings is the bar, and a finding fired at a part that\n   prints fine is a bug in the rule, not something to accept quietly.\n5. **Predict a baseline before you look at it.** Working out what the sliver count\n   should be from the polish exclusions turns the number into a test of the rule instead\n   of something to write down.\n6. **Render it and actually look.** `nurb render <part>` writes a PNG, `--section\n   z:4mm` cuts it open where outside views cannot reach, and `nurb inspect --render`\n   stands a camera at every finding with the guilty face painted. A part can pass\n   every numeric check and still be visibly wrong.\n\n===== API =====\n  from nurb import *  gives a part file all of build123d, plus:\n\n  part(fn)\n      Mark a function as a part.\n  reject(message, param=None)\n      Refuse to build a configuration the part knows cannot work.\n  is_convex(edge, f1, f2)\n      True if the material folds away from itself across this edge.\n  concave_edges(shape)\n      Every concave edge, which is where polish is forbidden and stress collects.\n  measured(name, start=None)\n      A measured value, in mm.\n  polish(shape, edges, size)\n      Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.\n  crown(wall, radius=None)\n      Round the top of a closed perimeter wall with one smooth bead, on request.\n  stand(shape, tilt=45.0, axis=Axis((0, 0, 0), (0, 1, 0)), facet=2.0, fins=True)\n      Tilt a part for diagonal printing and shave a flat for it to stand on.\n  counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0)\n      The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n  assembly(fn)\n      Mark a function as an assembly. Its keyword defaults are parameters, exactly\n  use(name, **overrides)\n      Build a sibling part by name and return its solid, placed where it was modelled.\n  hinge(solid, axis, through, at=0.0, name=None, step=3.0)\n      Declare that this solid rotates about `axis`, and pose it at `at` degrees.\n  obstacle(solid, name='an obstacle')\n      Context geometry: the wall, the machine, the shelf above.\n\n  And it replaces these build123d names with its own. Read the summary:\n\n  chamfer(objects: build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.zero_d.Vertex | collections.abc.Iterable[build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.zero_d.Vertex], length: float, length2: float | None = None, angle: float | None = None, reference: build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.two_d.Face | None = None) -> build123d.topology.composite.Sketch | build123d.topology.composite.Part\n      Generic Operation: chamfer\n  import_stl(file_name, units=None)\n      A closed, flat-faced STL as a real solid, or a refusal that names `nurb scan`.\n\n  From build123d, because the doctrine mandates it:\n\n  new_edges(*objects: 'Shape', combined: 'Shape') -> 'ShapeList[Edge]'\n      Given a sequence of shapes and the combination of those shapes, find the newly added edges\n\n  `nurb rules` is when to reach for these. `nurb inspect <part>` measures a\n  built one in the same units the rules report.\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"Bash","output":[],"output_for_prompt":"exit: 0 [truncated: showing first/last 21.9 KB of 34.9 KB - full output at: /var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-grok-home-820r72a7/sessions/%2Fprivate%2Fvar%2Ffolders%2Fs4%2Fby90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn%2FT%2Fnurb-eval-trial-7g7y7rfy%2Fproject/01a03587-081c-72b2-90bf-91bf5e12fe48/terminal/call-7333225b-fe9d-4058-bc66-c1eaaf87a222-3.log]\n# nurb design doctrine\n\nPrinted via `nurb rules`. This file is the single source; harness files point at it\nrather than repeating it.\n\nThese are standing rules for FDM-printed parts, and several are vetoes rather than\npreferences. Most of them are physics, so they outlive any particular CAD kernel. The\nones that are specific to OCCT are marked as such and were measured, not reasoned.\n\n## The part contract\n\n```python\nfrom nurb import *\n\n@part\ndef dispenser(width=80.0, height=120.0, wall=2.0, draft=False):\n    body = Box(width, height, wall)\n    if draft:\n        return body\n    bed = body.bounding_box().min.Z\n    keep = body.edges().filter_by(lambda e: e.bounding_box().min.Z > bed)\n    return polish(body, keep, 1.0)\n```\n\nKeyword defaults are the parameters. That one declaration feeds the agent, the CLI, the\nviewer, the tests, and any future configurator. Never add a parallel `PARAMS` dict; the\ntwo would drift.\n\n**Write a continuous dimension as a float.** The type of a default is the only thing\nthat says whether a value is a count or a measurement, and the viewer reads it: an `int`\ndefault gets a slider that steps by one, a `float` default one that moves continuously.\n`bracket_count=4` is a count and `chamfer_size=1.0` is a millimetre, so the trailing\n`.0` is doing work. Nothing else has to be declared, and there is no annotation to keep\nin sync, because the type is already there in the signature.\n\n`draft` is optional and injected by the runtime, never passed by a caller. When it is\ntrue, skip the polish pass. It is worth about 20% on a real part, not the 18x a cube\nsuggests.\n\nReturn one solid. A function that returns two loose bodies still reports a plausible\nbounding box and still exports, so nothing downstream catches it.\n\n**When a configuration cannot work, refuse it with `reject`, never a bare raise.** A holder whose hole is narrower than the tool it holds should not build, and `reject(\"drill_hole_width 14 is under the 14.27mm pin vise plus print shrink: raise it above 14.77\", param=\"drill_hole_width\")` is how a part says so. The message states what is wrong and what value fixes it; `param` names the offending parameter so the viewer marks its slider. The viewer presents a refusal as a limit of the design, in amber with the last good geometry still on screen, where a bare `ValueError` arrives as a red traceback that reads as the part being broken. Guard only what would print wrong or not work at all; taste stays a slider.\n\n## Printability\n\n- **Minimum wall 2 to 3mm**, except where fit geometry says otherwise. Fit-critical\n  dimensions are already tuned; do not round them for tidiness.\n- **Print support-free. No floating features.** Parts print bottom down, building up in\n  +z, so every functional feature is grounded. A vertical extrusion or a vertical\n  through hole is self-supporting. A shelf, loop, or band floated partway up with a gap\n  underneath is not.\n- **A counterbore prints mouth toward the bed, and that floats its ceiling.** The shelf the screw head bears on is laid flat over the open pocket, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air; the `hole_ceiling` finding is this exact case, and it appears on any hole rising out of a bridged roof, not just screw pockets. Never answer it with supports inside a pocket nobody can clean: cut the hole with `counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth)`, which steps the transition through two sacrificial bridge layers, a slot bridged chord-to-chord across the pocket and the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first, so each layer spans only what the one before it laid and the whole stack prints support-free. Reach for it whenever a design wants a screw head, a nut, or any wider recess on the bed side of a hole.\n- **Corbel rule.** Grounded does not mean a column to the bed. Support-free means no\n  layer overhanging past 45 degrees. Where material would run to the plate only to\n  satisfy printing, carry the feature on a 45 degree underside instead: a short vertical\n  tip of 4 to 6mm below the floor at the front face, then a 45 degree plane falling back\n  into the body. Every layer then overhangs by at most half a bead and bonds to the wall\n  behind it.\n- **Corbels are always exactly 45 degrees**, the same as every other facet. Never\n  steepen one or match it to a ratio. Let the landing point fall where 45 degrees puts\n  it. Mismatched facet angles are a veto: every facet on the part, cosmetic or\n  structural or corbel, has to read as one system.\n- **Elephant's foot is a slicer setting**, not a CAD problem. Do not model around it.\n\n### Fasteners\n\n**A published standard is not a guess.** Measurements says never improvise a dimension, and a screw is the one place that rule gets misread: nobody has to measure an M3 cap screw, because ISO fixed its head at 5.5mm across in every box you will ever open. The stance to refuse is the tent, elbow up, one leg's tip hanging in air, and it is easy to miss by eye because nothing about it looks steep: every face of the floating tip can sit at exactly 45 degrees, silent t\n\n... A printer is really a fact about the workshop,\n  not the project, so `~/.config/nurb/config.toml` takes the same schema and covers every\n  project on the machine; `printer.toml` overrides it where they disagree.\n\n```toml\n[part]\nmin_wall = 1.0\nforward = [-1, 0, 0]\n\n[accepted]\nsliver = 6\n```\n\n### Variants\n\nA catalog entry that is the same function at different numbers is a variant, not a new\nfile. It goes in the same fence, and `build`, `check`, `card` and `export` all walk it\nexactly as they walk the part's own defaults, so it gets its own STL, its own baselines\nand its own line in the AUTO block.\n\n```toml\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2]\nnote = \"The same shelf sized for the wide bin: three columns instead of two.\"\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.params]\ngrid_x = 3\nbracket_count = 6\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\nsliver = 26\n```\n\nThe test is whether it is the same geometry. A wider cradle on the same J is a variant.\nA different mechanism is a part, however similar the slab looks.\n\n`note` is one sentence saying why the variant exists, written in the user's words rather than the doctrine's, because the viewer shows it next to the variant and its reader is whoever is about to print the thing. The params are the how and never the why: `diagonal = true` explains nothing to someone who has not read the doctrine, and \"prints tilted so it needs no supports\" does.\n\n## Measurements\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off is not geometry, it is dimensions. A wrong number produces a\nperfect model of the wrong object, and nothing downstream can catch it.\n\n**Ask for a measurement. Never improvise one.** A guessed clearance that happens to\nbuild is worse than a failure, because it prints. Record what you are told in\n`measurements.toml` at the project root, with how it was obtained, and read it with\n`measured()`:\n\n```toml\n[shelf_depth]\nvalue = 340\nunit = \"mm\"\nhow = \"tape measure, freezer middle shelf, 2026-07-20\"\n```\n\n```python\nfrom nurb import measured\n\ndepth = measured(\"shelf_depth\")\n```\n\nAn unknown name raises, naming what is on file. That failure is the point: it is the\nmoment to ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n\n**A published number is a measurement, not a guess.** When the part mates with a manufactured product or a published standard, a VESA mount, a gridfinity bin, a camera thread, the number already exists in a datasheet, and making somebody caliper a hole pattern a standards body fixed is friction wearing rigor's clothes. Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. Pixels carry no millimetres, so a dimension read off a photo is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n\n**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n\n**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\nThe danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\nmonths later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\nnext person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\nsomebody picks up a caliper.\n\nA measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\namortized it over sixteen parts.\n\n## Assemblies\n\nEvery rule above judges one solid being manufactured. An assembly judges solids being\nused together: a door on its mount, a lid over the machine it covers. The failure it\nexists for built clean, checked clean, exported watertight and printed beautifully,\nthen jammed at 45 degrees of its swing, because the collision between two correct\nparts existed only in the physical world. No per-solid rule can have an opinion about\nmotion.\n\nThe contract mirrors a part's. An assembly is a function in `parts/` whose keyword\ndefaults are its parameters; what it returns is placed solids rather than one solid.\n`use(name)` builds a sibling part where it was modelled, `hinge(solid, axis, through,\nat)` declares a revolute joint and poses it, and `obstacle(solid, name)` adds what the\nparts mount into: the machine, the wall, the shelf above. The joint angle is an\nordinary keyword default, so the viewer's slider swings it live, and the slider ends\nexactly where the declared range does.\n\n**The declared range is a claim, and `nurb check` audits it** the way min_wall audits\na wall: each hinge sweeps through it against everything else in the scene, and a\nfinding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\nthe design needs, never what happens to pass. A door that must stay open by gravity\nneeds past 90 degrees; declaring (0, 90) because that sweeps clean is writing the test\nto match the bug.\n\n**Nothing full width may cross the pivot line.** The mount that carries a hinge has to\nreach the pin from somewhere, so some of it always lives above or behind the pin, and\nany full-width material on the moving part that reaches past the pivot sweeps straight\ninto it. The door that jammed at 45 did so against its own top edge, carried past the\npin to support the knuckles; the knuckles got narrow tabs instead, sized to pass\nbeside the mount, and the panel now starts at the pivot line.\n\n**Obstacles are boxes and prisms, never thin hand-drawn profiles.** Three nearly\ncollinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\nsolid with chunks of the other operand: measured once as a 152,000mm3 phantom\ncollision at a pose that was actually clear. The sweep refuses any intersection that\nis not a subset of its inputs and names the solid to fix, but the fix is to model the\nobstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n\n**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\ntwo faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\nIn plastic a zero-clearance pass is already a bind. The declared range should carry\nthe same honesty about clearance that a tongue's `fit` carries about width.\n\n**The stop the sweep finds can be the detent the design wants.** The door that\nmotivated all of this rests open at 240 degrees against the back of its own mount,\nheld there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\nto engineer away. Read a jam before redesigning around it.\n\n**Never trust a swing worked out by hand.** The same hinge was hand-modelled three\ntimes and gave three answers, one of them \"impossible\"; the wrong one ran a convex\ntest against a non-convex profile, which is exactly the kind of quiet modelling error\na kernel boolean cannot make. The sweep is the authority, and it is cheap.\n\nOne hinge sweeps at a time; the others hold their pose, which is the conservative\nreading of a mechanism you can only move one hand at a time. Printability rules stay\noff assemblies entirely: each part already answered them alone, and overhang measured\non an assembled scene reports confident nonsense.\n\n## Verification\n\n\"It built\" is not verification. `nurb verify` runs the machine-checkable part of\nthis list: one solid per configuration, every count flexed upward, the rules clean,\nand the card agreeing with the geometry. `--report` writes the verdict into\n`build/renders/<part>.verify.md` with the renders that back it beside it, one still\nper finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n\nBefore presenting a part:\n\n1. **Flex the driving parameters up as well as down**, for example 4 to 6 to 2 to 4.\n   Growth is what catches a frozen selector; shrinking alone passes a broken part.\n2. **Check fit-critical faces by coordinate** after any polish or resize. `nurb inspect`\n   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. Which faces are\n   fit-critical is still yours to know, which is why this is not something `nurb verify`\n   can run for you.\n3. **Confirm the solid count**, which is almost always one.\n4. **Run `nurb check`.** Zero findings is the bar, and a finding fired at a part that\n   prints fine is a bug in the rule, not something to accept quietly.\n5. **Predict a baseline before you look at it.** Working out what the sliver count\n   should be from the polish exclusions turns the number into a test of the rule instead\n   of something to write down.\n6. **Render it and actually look.** `nurb render <part>` writes a PNG, `--section\n   z:4mm` cuts it open where outside views cannot reach, and `nurb inspect --render`\n   stands a camera at every finding with the guilty face painted. A part can pass\n   every numeric check and still be visibly wrong.\n\n===== API =====\n  from nurb import *  gives a part file all of build123d, plus:\n\n  part(fn)\n      Mark a function as a part.\n  reject(message, param=None)\n      Refuse to build a configuration the part knows cannot work.\n  is_convex(edge, f1, f2)\n      True if the material folds away from itself across this edge.\n  concave_edges(shape)\n      Every concave edge, which is where polish is forbidden and stress collects.\n  measured(name, start=None)\n      A measured value, in mm.\n  polish(shape, edges, size)\n      Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.\n  crown(wall, radius=None)\n      Round the top of a closed perimeter wall with one smooth bead, on request.\n  stand(shape, tilt=45.0, axis=Axis((0, 0, 0), (0, 1, 0)), facet=2.0, fins=True)\n      Tilt a part for diagonal printing and shave a flat for it to stand on.\n  counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth, layer=1.0)\n      The negative space of a counterbored hole whose ceiling prints without support.\n  assembly(fn)\n      Mark a function as an assembly. Its keyword defaults are parameters, exactly\n  use(name, **overrides)\n      Build a sibling part by name and return its solid, placed where it was modelled.\n  hinge(solid, axis, through, at=0.0, name=None, step=3.0)\n      Declare that this solid rotates about `axis`, and pose it at `at` degrees.\n  obstacle(solid, name='an obstacle')\n      Context geometry: the wall, the machine, the shelf above.\n\n  And it replaces these build123d names with its own. Read the summary:\n\n  chamfer(objects: build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.zero_d.Vertex | collections.abc.Iterable[build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.zero_d.Vertex], length: float, length2: float | None = None, angle: float | None = None, reference: build123d.topology.one_d.Edge | build123d.topology.two_d.Face | None = None) -> build123d.topology.composite.Sketch | build123d.topology.composite.Part\n      Generic Operation: chamfer\n  import_stl(file_name, units=None)\n      A closed, flat-faced STL as a real solid, or a refusal that names `nurb scan`.\n\n  From build123d, because the doctrine mandates it:\n\n  new_edges(*objects: 'Shape', combined: 'Shape') -> 'ShapeList[Edge]'\n      Given a sequence of shapes and the combination of those shapes, find the newly added edges\n\n  `nurb rules` is when to reach for these. `nurb inspect <part>` measures a\n  built one in the same units the rules report.\n","exit_code":0,"command":"nurb rules && echo \"===== API =====\" && nurb api","truncated":true,"signal":null,"timed_out":false,"description":"Print nurb rules and API vocabulary","current_dir":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-7g7y7rfy/project","output_file":"/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-grok-home-820r72a7/sessions/%2Fprivate%2Fvar%2Ffolders%2Fs4%2Fby90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn%2FT%2Fnurb-eval-trial-7g7y7rfy%2Fproject/01a03587-081c-72b2-90bf-91bf5e12fe48/terminal/call-7333225b-fe9d-4058-bc66-c1eaaf87a222-3.log","total_bytes":35779},"locations":[]}

The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the most common way a\n# part stops building, so it is the one message worth owning. `fillet` is deliberately\n# not wrapped: build123d's fillet error already names `max_fillet()` and a smaller\n# radius really is the fix there, so the chamfer rule would be wrong half the time.\n# `import_stl` shadows build123d's for a blunter reason: build123d's returns a `Face`,\n# a sheet of triangles with no inside, and subtracting from one segfaults instead of\n# raising. A part builds inside the `nurb dev` process, so the whole watcher dies\n60→# without printing anything. It is also the first name a model tries when a downloaded\n# file lands in a project. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-82484218-1b2d-44bc-8faf-9ff20672f62b-17","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"1→\"\"\"Printability rules, run against the solid rather than against an export.\n\nChecking the B-rep instead of an STL means real faces with exact areas and normals\ninstead of triangles, which is what makes most of these rules cheap and exact.\n\nA rule takes the shape and a Context and yields Findings. Rules compose: they never\nsee each other, and `run` gathers them.\n\"\"\"\n\n10→import pathlib\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass, field, replace\nfrom math import asin, atan2, cos, degrees, pi, sin\n\nfrom build123d import Axis, CenterOf, GeomType, Vector\n\nFAIL = \"fail\"  # this will not print, or will not work\nWARN = \"warn\"  # this needs attention, most often support\n\n# What each rule is called to somebody who owns a printer rather than to whoever wrote\n20→# the rule. A rule's name is an identifier: it is stable, it is what the CLI prints, and\n# it is what an agent matches on, so it never changes. But the person reading the viewer\n# came here to get a part printed, and `concave_cosmetic` tells them nothing they can\n# act on. Both are true at once, which is why this is a translation and not a rename.\n# Kept short because the panel gives them a fixed column: a label that wraps puts two\n# ragged lines next to a message that is already wrapping. LABEL_WIDTH is what fits.\nLABEL_WIDTH = 14\nLABELS = {\n    # Covers both of this rule's states, which is why it is not \"loose pieces\": the\n    # same rule fires on a part that came apart and on one that built nothing at all.\n30→    \"solids\": \"not one piece\",\n    \"overhang\": \"steep slope\",\n    \"floating\": \"starts on air\",\n    \"hole_ceiling\": \"hole on air\",\n    \"min_wall\": \"thin wall\",\n    \"sliver\": \"tiny detail\",\n    \"concave_cosmetic\": \"inside corner\",\n    \"bed_bevel\": \"poor bed grip\",\n    \"stability\": \"tips over\",\n    \"projection_ratio\": \"long reach\",\n40→    \"build_volume\": \"too big\",\n}\n\n\ndef label(rule):\n    \"\"\"What to call a rule on screen. Falls back to the identifier, which is honest:\n    a rule with no label is a gap to fill, not a reason to show nothing.\"\"\"\n    return LABELS.get(rule, rule)\n\n\n50→@dataclass(frozen=True)\nclass Finding:\n    \"\"\"What a rule found, said twice.\n\n    `message` is written for whoever is going to fix it: exact, dense, and in the\n    vocabulary of the doctrine, because an agent reads it and then edits geometry.\n    `plain` is the same fact for whoever is looking at the part, who came here to print\n    something and has never needed the word \"facet\".\n\n    Two strings rather than one because the audiences want opposite things. \"1.4mm strip\n60→    sitting in a concave junction over 42.4mm2\" is the right sentence for the reader who\n    is about to move that face, and the wrong one for the reader deciding whether to\n    press print. Where a message is already plain, `plain` stays None and both get it.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    rule: str\n    severity: str\n    message: str\n    value: float | None = None\n    where: tuple | None = None\n70→    plain: str | None = None\n\n    @property\n    def label(self):\n        return label(self.rule)\n\n    @property\n    def said(self):\n        \"\"\"The sentence to show a person.\"\"\"\n        return self.plain or self.message\n80→\n    def __str__(self):\n        spot = f\"  at ({self.where[0]:.1f}, {self.where[1]:.1f}, {self.where[2]:.1f})\" if self.where else \"\"\n        return f\"{self.severity:4}  {self.rule:20} {self.message}{spot}\"\n\n\n@dataclass\nclass Context:\n    \"\"\"What a rule needs to know beyond the geometry itself.\n\n90→    `up` is the build direction, which is the model's z only when a part is printed\n    the way it is modelled. Notch parts are, so +z is right for them. Empty when there is no file.\"\"\"\n    path = _find(pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2))\n    return _read(path) if path else {}\n\n\ndef provisional(start=None):\n    \"\"\"Every value on file that admits it was not actually measured.\n\n    The doctrine says to ask rather than improvise, and sometimes there is nobody to\n    ask: the caliper is in a drawer, the object is at the office, the print has to go\n    on tonight. The failure mode is not putting a number in, it is that the number then\n    looks exactly like one somebody measured. Six months later nothing distinguishes\n    them and the part is wrong for a reason no one can find.\n\n    So a guess is allowed to be written down and required to say so, and `nurb check`\n    reports the ones that still are. `how` stays mandatory either way: a provisional\n    value still has to say where it came from, because \"eyeballed against a broom\"\n    tells the next person how much to trust it.\n    \"\"\"\n    return sorted(\n        (name, entry.get(\"how\", \"\"))\n        for name, entry in load(start).items()\n","total_lines":126}},"locations":[]}

The near miss is worse\n    than the obvious one. A shelf that only touches its post along a tangent line reads\n    as joined in the viewer from every angle, and the kernel quietly keeps it as two.\n\n    What makes this the one rule that silences the others is that every rule after it\n    reads `shape.solids()[0]`. The part may turn on the plate,\n        # but rolling it onto another axis would put the facet in the air again.\n        bed, top = _span(shape, up)\n        height = top - bed\n        footprint = sorted(\n            _span(shape, axis)[1] - _span(shape, axis)[0]\n            for axis in _bed_axes(up)\n        )\n        plate = sorted(ctx.bed[:2])\n        got = [height, *footprint]\n        fits = height <= ctx.bed[2] and (\n            all(got <= available for got, available in zip(footprint, plate))\n            or _fits_rotated(shape, ctx.bed, up)\n        )\n        orientation = \"in its fixed build orientation\"\n    if fits:\n        return []\n    return [\n        Finding(\n            \"build_volume\",\n            FAIL,\n            f\"{size.X:.0f} x {size.Y:.0f} x {size.Z:.0f}mm does not fit \"\n            f\"{ctx.bed[0]:.0f} x {ctx.bed[1]:.0f} x {ctx.bed[2]:.0f}mm {orientation}\",\n            value=round(max(got), 1),\n        )\n    ]\n\n\ndef _sample_normals(face, grid=5):\n    \"\"\"Outward normals across a face.\n\n    A planar face has one and it is exact. A curved face does not, and the sampling\n    has to reach the ends of its parameter range: sampling cell midpoints instead\n    walks a cylinder round at 45, 135, 225 and 315 degrees and never once looks\n    straight down, which is the only normal an overhang check cares about. Planar\n    faces, which is nearly all of this library, are exact.\n    \"\"\"\n    if face.geom_type == GeomType.PLANE:\n        return [face.normal_at(face.center())]\n    out = []\n    for i in range(grid):\n        for j in range(grid):\n            try:\n                spot = face.position_at(i / (grid - 1), j / (grid - 1))\n                out.append(face.normal_at(spot))\n            except Exception:\n                continue\n    return out or [face.normal_at(face.center())]\n\n\ndef _span(shape, up):\n    \"\"\"How far a shape reaches along the build direction, as (lowest, highest).\n\n    Off the bounding box, not off the vertices. A curved face has vertices only where\n    its seam is, so a cylinder's lowest vertex sits at its middle: measuring from\n    vertices put the bed 8mm above the actual bottom of a cylinder, marked every face\n    grounded, and turned the overhang rule off without any sign that it had happened.\n\n","total_lines":1428}},"locations":[]}

Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. Suggested code has to echo back the float the caller passed.\n            f\"  polish(shape, edges, {size!r}) bisects the set for you and keeps the halves that\",\n            \"  build, which is what the doctrine prescribes instead of narrowing by hand.\",\n        ]\n    else:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            \"  A single edge that fails at every length is the other case: its end lands on\",\n            \"  a vertex with four faces and only three edges between them, and OCCT has no\",\n            \"  cap for that corner. Chamfer in two passes and reselect from the result.\",\n        ]\n    lines += [\"\", \"  `nurb rules` has both cases in full.\"]\n    return \"\\n\".join(lines)\n\n\ndef _count(objects):\n    try:\n        return len(list(objects))\n    except TypeError:  # a single Edge or Vertex, which is not iterable\n        return 1\n\n\ndef _guarded(fn, word, objects, size, *args, **kwargs):\n    # A generator would be exhausted by the failing call, so the count in the message\n    # would read zero and the advice would describe the wrong case. It is a real number\n    that builds a real part; what it is not is measured, and `nurb check` says so until\n    somebody picks up a caliper.\n    \"\"\"\n    start = pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2)\n    path = _find(start)\n    if path is None:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"no {FILENAME} above {start}, so there is nothing measured to ask for. \"\n            f\"Measure {name!r}, then record it:\\n\\n\"\n            f'  [{name}]\\n  value = 0\\n  unit = \"mm\"\\n  how = \"how you measured it\"\\n\\n'\n            f\"If there is nobody to ask right now, add `provisional = true` and put \"\n            f\"your best guess in. `nurb check` will keep reminding you.\"\n        )\n    book = _read(path)\n    if name not in book:\n        have = \", \".join(sorted(book)) or \"nothing yet\"\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"nothing measured called {name!r} in {path.name}. have: {have}. \"\n            f\"Ask for the measurement rather than guessing one: a wrong number here \"\n            f\"builds, checks clean, and prints.\"\n        )\n    entry = book[name]\n    if \"value\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} has no value\")\n    if \"how\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"{path.name}: {name} does not say how it was measured. Deciding which edges are exposed, which are\n    mating geometry and which are concave is the part's judgement and stays in the part.\n    \"\"\"\n    floor = 0.8 * size**2\n\n    def tiny(solid):\n        return len([f for f in solid.faces() if f.area < floor])\n\n    allowance = tiny(shape)\n    kept, best = [], shape\n\n    def lands(candidate):\n        try:\n            # The raw kernel call, not the guarded one: bisecting expects failures and\n            # discards them, so building the guidance text here would be advice nobody\n            # reads, once per attempt.\n            out = _chamfer(candidate, size)\n        except Exception:\n            return None\n        return out if tiny(out) <= allowance else None\n\n    def take(batch):\n        nonlocal kept, best\n        out = lands(kept + batch)\n        if out is not None:\n            kept, best = kept + batch, out\n            return\n        if len(batch) == 1:\n            return  # this one cannot land beside what is already there\n        half = len(batch) // 2\n        take(batch[:half])\n        take(batch[half:])\n\n    take(sorted(edges, key=lambda e: (-e.length, e.center().X, e.center().Y, e.center().Z)))\n    return best\n","total_lines":153}},"locations":[]}

Only\n    meaningful for something cantilevered off a wall, so a part opts in by saying\n    which way it reaches.\n    \"\"\"\n    reaching = projection(shape, ctx)\n    if reaching is None:\n        return []\n    reach, height, ratio = reaching\n    if ratio <= ctx.projection_limit:\n        return []\n    return [\n        Finding(\n            \"projection_ratio\",\n            WARN,\n            f\"reaches {reach:.0f}mm on a {height:.0f}mm back, ratio {ratio:.2f}. \"\n            f\"past {ctx.projection_limit:.1f} the fix is a taller back, not more gusset\",\n            plain=f\"sticks out {reach:.0f}mm from a mount only {height:.0f}mm tall. \"\n            \"That leverage sags under load, or snaps\",\n            value=round(ratio, 2),\n        )\n    ]\n\n\n@rule(\"bed_bevel\")\ndef bed_bevel(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Polish laid on the edges that meet the build plate.\n\n    A chamfer down there buys nothing and costs a mess: it turns the first layer into\n    a knife edge that squashes, and on a mount-facing part it stops the thing sitting\n    flat.\n\n    A tilted face touching the bed is not always a bevel, which this rule assumed and\n    the calipers holder disproved. The doctrine's corbel is a 45 degree underside, and\n    where it lands on the plate rather than dying into the body it is exactly such a\n    face: structure, prescribed, and 46mm2 of it.\n\n    What separates them is how far the face rises off the bed, and a bottom chamfer's\n    rise is its size exactly. Measured: 1.00, 2.00 and 3.00mm for chamfers of those\n    sizes, against 4.29mm for the corbel. Rise is independent of the edge's path: a 1mm\n    chamfer around a 20mm cylinder is still 1mm high even though its plan-view bounding\n    box is 20mm in both directions. The limit is three times the polish size because the\n    largest chamfer this doctrine puts anywhere is the 3mm structural relief, and the\n    smallest corbel starts with a 4 to 6mm vertical tip.\n    \"\"\"\n    up = Vector(*ctx.up).normalized()\n    bed, _ = _span(shape, up)\n    if _standing(shape, up) is not None:\n        # Standing on a facet, so every face at the plate is deliberately tilted and\n        # there is no bottom whose rim a bevel could dress. A 4mm wall stood at 45\n        # rises 2.83mm, square in the chamfer band, and prints fine: rise cannot tell\n        # it from a bevel, but the stance can.\n        return []\n    found = []\n    for face in shape.faces():\n        low, high = _span(face, up)\n        if low > bed + 1e-4:\n            continue  # does not reach the plate\n        if high - low > 3 * ctx.cosmetic_chamfer:\n            continue  # rises too far to be a chamfer band, so it is structure\n        for normal in _sample_normals(face):\n            tilt = abs(normal.dot(up))\n            if 0.03 < tilt < 0.97:  # neither lying on the plate nor standing on it\n                found.append(\n                    Finding(\n                        \"bed_bevel\",\n                        WARN,\n                        f\"{degrees(asin(min(1.0, tilt))):.0f}deg face meets the build \"\n                        f\"plate over {face.area:.1f}mm2, which is polish on the first layer\",\n                        plain=f\"a chamfer along {face.area:.0f}mm2 of the bottom edge, \"\n                        \"where it costs the first layer its grip on the plate\",\n                        value=round(face.area, 2),\n                        where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in face.center()),\n                    )\n                )\n                break\n    return found\n\n\n@rule(\"concave_cosmetic\")\ndef concave_cosmetic(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Polish laid into an inside corner instead of across an outside one.\n\n","total_lines":1428}},"locations":[]}

Must sit strictly\n# between the grader's 0.3mm \"must pass\" growth and 1.0mm \"must jam\" growth.\n_BORE_CLEARANCE = 0.75\n\n_KNOB_LENGTH = 40.0\n_KNOB_WIDTH = 30.0\n_KNOB_HEIGHT = 14.0\n\n\ndef _d_face(diameter, across_flat):\n    \"\"\"D-profile on XY, circle centered on the origin, flat facing +X.\"\"\"\n    radius = diameter / 2.0\n    flat_x = across_flat - radius\n    half = sqrt(radius * radius - flat_x * flat_x)\n    start = degrees(atan2(half, flat_x))\n    end = degrees(atan2(-half, flat_x))\n    arc = Edge.make_circle(\n        radius,\n        Plane.XY,\n        start_angle=start,\n        end_angle=end + 360.0,\n        angular_direction=AngularDirection.COUNTER_CLOCKWISE,\n    )\n    line = Edge.make_line(Vector(flat_x, -half, 0), Vector(flat_x, half, 0))\n    return Face(Wire.combine([arc, line]))\n\n\n@part\ndef valve_knob(\n    shaft_diameter=measured(\"shaft_diameter\"),\n    shaft_across_flat=measured(\"shaft_across_flat\"),\n    draft=False,\n):\n    \"\"\"Replacement knob for a valve with a D-shaped stem. Prints bore-up.\n\n    shaft_diameter: round-side width of the stem the knob drops onto\n    shaft_across_flat: stem thickness from the flat to the opposite round side\n    \"\"\"\n    if shaft_diameter <= 0:\n        reject(\"shaft_diameter must be positive\", param=\"shaft_diameter\")\n    if shaft_across_flat <= 0:\n        reject(\"shaft_across_flat must be positive\", param=\"shaft_across_flat\")\n    if shaft_across_flat >= shaft_diameter:\n        reject(\n            f\"shaft_across_flat {shaft_across_flat}mm must be under \"\n            f\"shaft_diameter {shaft_diameter}mm to leave a driving flat\",\n            param=\"shaft_across_flat\",\n        )\n\n    bore_diameter = shaft_diameter + _BORE_CLEARANCE\n    bore_across_flat = shaft_across_flat + _BORE_CLEARANCE\n    if bore_across_flat >= bore_diameter:\n        reject(\n            \"shaft_across_flat is too close to shaft_diameter for the bore clearance\",\n            param=\"shaft_across_flat\",\n        )\n    if bore_diameter + 4.0 > _KNOB_WIDTH:\n        reject(\n            f\"shaft_diameter {shaft_diameter}mm is too large for this knob body\",\n            param=\"shaft_diameter\",\n        )\n\n    body = extrude(SlotOverall(_KNOB_LENGTH, _KNOB_WIDTH), amount=_KNOB_HEIGHT)\n    bore = extrude(_d_face(bore_diameter, bore_across_flat), amount=_KNOB_HEIGHT + 2.0)\n    body = body - bore.moved(Location(0, 0, -1.0))\n\n    if draft:\n        return body\n\n    bed = body.bounding_box().min.Z\n    concave = set(concave_edges(body))\n    bore_limit = (bore_diameter / 2.0) + 0.8\n\n    def polishable(edge):\n        if edge in concave:\n            return False\n        if edge.bounding_box().min.Z <= bed + 1e-4:\n            return False\n        center = edge.center()\n        if center.X * center.X + center.Y * center.Y < bore_limit * bore_limit:\n            return False\n        return True\n\n    keep = body.edges().filter_by(polishable)\n    return polish(body, keep, 1.0)\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

Prints mouth-up and\nflips onto the stem in use.\n\n## What it is\n\nA 40 × 30 × 14mm stadium-shaped knob with a D-bore on the vertical centerline,\nflat facing +X. The bore is derived from `shaft_diameter` and `shaft_across_flat`.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- Prints bore-up: the D-hole is a vertical extrusion, support-free, on a 14mm\n  first-layer footprint.\n- In use the part flips over so the print-top mouth drops onto the 12mm stem.\n- Bore is 0.75mm oversize on both diameter and across-flat: a stem grown 0.3mm\n  drops in; a stem grown 1.0mm binds; a 20° twist of the 0.3mm stem hits the flat.\n- Stadium outline gives a 30mm narrow width and a 20mm reach from the centerline\n  (about 33% past the half-width), so wet hands can turn it.\n- 1mm chamfer on exposed outer edges. The D-bore stays sharp so it still drives.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Don't round the bore into a circle: a round hole will not transmit torque.\n- Don't chamfer the bed edges or the D-bore: bed chamfers kill first-layer grip,\n  and a dressed bore fails the fit/torque checks.\n- Don't close the bore with a ceiling facing the bed; that is a hole-on-air.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- First model: stadium paddle, through D-bore, prints bore-up.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}
